Burger King's New Advent Calendar Has A Big Surprise Behind Its 12 Doors

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Last year's disappeared in minutes. Consider yourself warned.

Remember when Advent calendars were just little cardboard boxes with a piece of chocolate behind each door? Nowadays, you can find them filled with everything from beauty products to booze—and apparently, miniature fast food restaurants.

Burger King's Advent calendar is returning for a third year, and this time, you can practically open your own franchise.

If you remember last year's edition, it was packed with some pretty unexpected throwbacks, including Cini Mini-inspired candles, a tiny board game, and a JD the Dog plush. Fans ate it up, too, as Burger King said its last two calendars sold out in less than five minutes.

For 2026, the chain has ditched the assortment of random collectibles for something you can actually put together. Joel Yashinsky, Burger King's chief marketing officer, said fans can “build their very own BK restaurant.”

Behind the 12 doors are miniature versions of just about everything you'd find at your local BK: There's a Whopper with its own box, fries, nuggets, a soda fountain, a booth, employees, and customers. And of course, who can forget the essential Christmas tree and a reindeer pairing, too.

The only thing missing is food you can actually eat, although the final compartment does include a $5 digital offer for eligible Royal Perks members.

Now, if you want to get your hands on one, you'll have five chances. Burger King is releasing just 110 calendars each day from November 16 through November 20, with sales starting at 12 p.m. ET exclusively at BKAdventCalendar.com.

Each costs $19.54, a reference to the year Burger King was founded, and orders are limited to one per household, with free standard shipping to the contiguous U.S. and Washington, D.C. If you manage to snag one of the Advent calendars, expect it to ship the week of November 30.

So have yourself a merry little Whopper this Christmas. And I do mean little.

TeamGroup GA Pro 2TB SSD Review: A faltering attempt at a mid-range PCIe 5.0 SSD

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The TeamGroup GA Pro is a drive that mostly misses. While it has relatively good points at QD8 and acceptable steady-state write speed, overall performance is very low with this inefficient, hot-running drive.

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Every once in a while we get a drive that comes across our desk that stands out, for good or for bad. The TeamGroup GA Pro falls into the latter category, enough so that we re-checked our results and contacted the company. We’ve managed to mostly sort out what’s going on and, while this is not the worst drive around, it has enough problems that we would generally not recommend it except if it's on steep sale.

TeamGroup’s array of storage offerings knows no bounds: if there’s a niche to cover, it makes an attempt. The GA Pro feels like such a try, although we’re not completely sure where it fits into the mix. The company is still working to iron out the bugs, if it can, but the technology is old enough that we wouldn’t hold our breath. On paper, it’s a PCIe 5.0 drive with DRAM that should beat any PCIe 4.0 SSD, but we found it struggles to live up to its own class of drive. Bright points are few and far between, but at least we can say it’s not the worst drive we’ve seen. The edges are just rougher than usual.

The TeamGroup GA Pro is available in 1TB, 2TB, 4TB, and 8TB capacities, although we only have the 2TB showing up at $331.99 at retail right now. The 8TB will probably be hard to find at this point. The drive is rated for up to 10,000 / 8,500 MB/s for sequential reads and writes, with no IOPS rating, although a drive like this can achieve 1 million or more. It has the standard five-year warranty with up to 600 TB of writes per TB of capacity.

TeamGroup has a number of downloads available for this range of drives, which is better than nothing. They do maintain firmware for their drives, and we made sure our test sample had the newest revision, 050g05E7. Also available is the SSD S.M.A.R.T. TOOL, which is their SSD toolbox with basic functions, such as health monitoring. For backup and cloning on Windows, we continue to recommend MultiDrive, which continues to be updated based on user feedback.

The good news is, the GA Pro has a graphene heat-spreading label. The bad news is, it’s insufficient to cool this drive. In most cases, you will want a heatsink on this. You can use a heatsink with the label in place, but you will have better luck if it’s removed. We are fond of graphene labels as they can be surprisingly effective, but it’s just not enough here.

Then we have the label which, with the 3.3V / 2.5A rating, puts the drive at a very optimistic 8.25W. We measured over 12.5W peak in our testing below. We can’t go by reported power states, as these top off at 3.50 W. Since we can’t rely on that, we can’t say for sure what even the expected enter/exit latencies are, relevant given TeamGroup’s claims about the drive, which we’ll get to later. The drive is otherwise double-sided with a controller, two DRAM packages, and four NAND flash packages.

The SSD controller is InnoGrit’s IG5666, the company’s answer to the Phison E26 high-end PCIe 5.0 champion. We have better controllers in that segment now, like the E28 and SMI’s SM2508, but even at the time of release, the IG5666 was not very popular. Both the E26 and IG5666 run very hot due to using an older process node, and newer flash wasn’t available yet to more easily reach the bus speeds needed. The GA Pro is not running full-out but, to be honest, that doesn’t help much. Phison put a lot of work into getting the E26 up to full speed in steps, and even the base 10 GB/s ones run toasty.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The drive does have the right amount of DDR4, 2GB, for this amount of flash. No problems there. The flash also looks to be YMTC’s 232-layer TLC, which is also perfectly fine. The controller is the weak spot here and, as we’ll find out, its firmware really keeps the GA Pro from reaching its full potential, even with its bandwidth cap.

Epic Games' Quixel Megascans has thousands of 3D models, decals and materials.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

No it's not a flashback to 2024, Epic Games has made its huge Quixel Megascans library free again, and this time it seems it's permanent. Access to the collection of photogrammetry-based 2D and 3D real-world assets was made free for a limited time two years ago to mark the launch of the Fab marketplace, but now it will be free indefinitely.

Quixel features thousands of production-ready items, including 3D models, tileable physically based rendering (PBR) surfaces, vegetation, decals, and custom materials, created by scanning real-world objects, environments. That makes it a massive resource for game developers, 3D artists, VFX artists and those working in architectural visualisation (also see our guides to the best laptops for CAD and the best laptops for 3D modelling).

The full Quixel Megascans library is going free. Fab listings will update between now & Nov 12, so check back if an asset you want isn’t free yet. We’ve updated terms for all Quixel assets so you can train AI models on Megascans & Megaplants. Learn more: https://t.co/jX1C4jRauw https://t.co/nuMDVwvJwcNovember 5, 2026

Megascans currently contains around 23,000 3D scanned assets. Quixel says listings on the Fab marketplace will be updated up until November 12 to make items free. It's also updated the terms for Quixel assets to allow Megascans and Megaplants assets to be used to train AI models.

"Your AI models can now learn from real-world scans to help you generate varied, photorealistic environments for your projects," it says.

Anyone who bought a Megascans or a Quixel Bundle on or after January 1, 2025 should receive a refund to their Epic Account Balance by the end of the year while keeping their licence and getting the new AI rights. The balance can be used across Epic's products, not just on Fab.

Epic has worked to make the new Fab marketplace as easy to use as possible; Channels can be used to discover compatible game engines while a mix of licences ensure you can find the right asset to meet your needs. Fab offers a Creative Commons licence and the Personal or Professional tier of a Standard License. Epic Games press release clarifies: "The Fab Standard License enables you to use the assets, including Megascans, in any game engine or tool you want."

Check out the Megascans library on Faby to see the variety of assets available, from furniture, and architecture to creature, vehicles and materials.

The Best Way to Eat Swiss Fondue? Floating on Lake Brienz

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

I could say that I was surprised to find myself and my family floating along in a red Zodiac on the deepest lake in Switzerland, eating cheese fondue and sipping white wine as the sun set. But really, it was all by design. When I designated myself as chief planner for an autumnal family trip to the Alpine nation with my adult children and their partners, I searched for an activity that would knock their socks off. I found it in Interlaken, with a floating fondue experience. Yes, you read that right.

I love Switzerland, and have visited the land where the hills are alive with the sound of cowbells more than 30 times in my life. And while I’ve done many amazing things in and around the Swiss Alps—skiing, hiking, trottinetting, whittling my own Swiss cow from a block of wood—I knew getting my Gen Z crew excited about family time involved finding something a little more unconventional.

We were going to be staying in the Bernese Oberland, a picture-postcard region with legendary sites like Grindelwald, the Jungfrau and Eiger mountains, and Interlaken. Everyone was up for hiking and taking cable cars to waterfalls; paragliding was sadly vetoed. But a unique culinary experience would surely work, I thought—the way to their hearts is through their stomachs.

Luckily, I had heard about a Cheese Fondue Float experience offered by a company called Outdoor Switzerland a few years ago. Back then, it was raclette—toasted cheese scraped onto boiled potatoes—that served as the dish du jour. But fondue, Switzerland’s national dish, is much more well-known, so the company savvily swapped raclette trays for caquelons (ceramic fondue pots). I found the experience listing on Get Your Guide, and reviews praised the change, highlighting the delicious cheese and wine and the gregarious fondue fun. I confirmed a private booking for the six of us a month before we left home, and kept my fingers crossed for good weather.

I needn’t have worried—my prayers were answered with glorious temperatures and bluebird skies on the day of the tour, a leisurely Sunday in September. A five-minute bus ride from downtown Interlaken brought us to the small village of Bonigen, the launch site located on scenic Lake Brienz. After shaking hands with our guide, a tall, friendly Dutchman named Hild, we donned life jackets, listened to the mandatory safety briefing, and stepped into the boat, alternating sides to keep the weight even. Once comfortably settled around the pretty red-and-white-checkered tablecloth, which was already topped with plates, cups, and two fondue pots, Hild slowly rowed us onto the lake.

An overhead shot of the pretty tablescape and gorgeous lake

Lake Brienz is the deepest lake in the country, and it’s surely one of the most scenic too. Moving out from the shore, we encountered spectacular views of towering mountains, their peaks mirrored in the impossibly blue water. I poured the prepared cheese fondue into the heated fondue pots, and the mixture began bubbling, filling the air with a sharp aroma as it warmed into readiness.

The fondue float experience includes white wine (one 500-ml bottle per person), as well as water and hot tea. We took turns pouring wine into our copper cups as my eldest son took command of the Bluetooth speaker, setting a lively mood with traditional Alpine tunes played on accordions and hackbretts (a kind of dulcimer), mixed with the occasional yodel ditty. At one point, our float’s music and laughter brought friendly waves from passengers on a passing cruise boat, along with toots from the ship’s horn. We whooped and waved in return.

Once the cheese was steaming hot, we dipped and swished bread cubes into the melted goodness, savoring the delicious alchemy of bread and cheese alongside celebratory toasts in honor of our family adventure. As the cheese disappeared, the fondue pot heated the remains into what the Swiss call “la réligieuse”—toasted cheese bits that are scraped from the bottom of the pot. We fought playfully over dibs on the last delicious morsels—trust me, they’re good to the last perfectly browned crumb.

Raising a glass to a unique family adventure

The entire experience lasted about an hour and a half, and during that time, the six of us managed to drink all six wine bottles on board. None of us are big drinkers, but the beauty of the surroundings inspired the good times to flow, and, well, so did the wine.

Photos and videos captured our cheesy adventure, but the memory of enjoying such a unique culinary and cultural experience with my favorite people in the world will be forever burned into my mind. You can have a fondue anywhere, but while floating in one of the most iconic locations in one of the most beautiful countries on earth? Even my discerning Gen Z travel companions agreed: “That’s extra.”

The Cheese Fondue Float experience on Lake Brienz in Interlaken is available to book on the Outdoor Switzerland website, as well as on GetYourGuide and Viator. It is offered between September and April, weather permitting. If booking in the colder months, bundle up with coats, beanies, and gloves, as you’ll be out on the water for up to an hour and a half. Private charters are available for groups up to eight people; kids eight years and up are welcome. More of a dessert person? Outdoor Switzerland also offers a Swiss Chocolate Fondue experience. Get cozy under blankets, sip hot tea, and dip marshmallows and fruit into creamy melted Swiss chocolate while a qualified guide rows you merrily along the beautiful Brienzersee.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Nov. 7, 1882: A Great Comet transforms astrophotography

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

In early September 1882, a comet was sighted from the Cape of Good Hope in South Africa. The comet brightened over the following weeks, reaching a magnitude of -2 on the 15th; at perihelion two days later, it was bright enough to be visible during the day. But at the time, David Gill, the director of the Cape Observatory, was busy observing minor planets Victoria and Sappho. It wasn’t until Oct. 12 that, his minor planet observations completed, he turned his attention to the comet and began photographing it.

Gill captured images of the Great Comet of 1882 on Oct. 12, 20, and 21, and Nov. 7, 13, and 14. The Nov. 7 shot was the one he would become famous for, though: It was not the first photograph of a comet, but rather would signal a change in astronomers’ uses of astrophotography. The photo was a 100-minute exposure, resulting an image that included stars to 10th magnitude and the comet’s 18-degree tail. Spurred by the large number of stars captured in his photo, Gill saw the potential for using astrophotography for star mapping and began the Cape Photographic Durchmusterung. (Durchmusterung means “close examination” or “scrutiny.”) The project would catalog nearly a half-million southern stars. Gill shared the successes of the program internationally, prompting the assembly of the world’s first major astronomical conference. As a result, astronomers worldwide began to move away from traditional, manual star mapping and towards more efficient and comprehensive photographic techniques.

Elisa Neckar is senior production editor of Astronomy magazine, and the editor of the Today in the History of Astronomy feature on Astronomy.com.

Poll: What Review Score Would You Give The Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Remake?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

It's here. It's finally here, and we'd wager you've spent a good chunk of the past 48+ hours playing it. Or perhaps you're savouring the moment, waiting for the perfect time to settle down with the most critically acclaimed game of the year (so far).

Either way, Ocarina is out on Switch 2, and millions of you have had the opportunity to dive into this beautifully reimagined version of a Hyrule that's near and dear to so many of us.

As mentioned, it's gone down very well with critics and currently sits with a whopping great Metascore of 96 after 85 reviews. Our own Ollie Reynolds called it "a remarkable remake, and a must-buy for Switch 2" in his 9/10 review:

"Ocarina of Time on Switch 2 is a stellar reimagining of the original, one that completely revamps the experience with stunning visuals, modernised gameplay, and vastly improved audio. Despite some minor blemishes, this is comfortably the most visually stunning work Nintendo has ever put out, and it’s unquestionably the best way to play one of the most seminal games of all time."

Review The Legend Of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time (Switch 2) - A Stunning Remake, But Not Quite Definitive

Now that the game has been out a couple of days, though, it's time to start canvassing public opinion: What score would you give this particular OoT?

No worries if you haven't played it yet or don't feel comfortable scoring it right now - you can return and rate the game at any time by revisiting this page or heading to our game page. As quickly as Ocarina veterans may have bolted through it, this is still a sizeable adventure, especially if you never played the original!

@N7Dan I just wanted to say how much I love your username. Here’s to Mass Effect miraculously coming to switch 2 one day… 🥂

OoT 2026 is a 10/10 for me. It is an experience, not a game. The tightrope balance of keeping the feeling (cinematic and scope) while still amazing (as in to make someone amazed) the player is being well kept. There are areas of the game that make me viscerally uncomfortable (or elated) even though I know they are there.

This game is what nostalgia truly means. Not some ruined or derided longing for better days, but the ability to have core memories and associations strummed by an evocative experience. This game does this as I find myself giggling or shuddering or laughing or trying not to tear up. At the same time it is FUN. I miss pure adventure games in a world of open world procedurals (BOTW and TOTK managed to avoid this feeling), get gud death runs, and epic rpgs where I need a spreadsheet to keep track of choices made so I get the ending I want. I just want to wander, and perhaps find some new connection.

I’m reminded of why I was so adamant that zelda games were adventure games and not RPGs as a kid (I still feel this way), I don't need to learn combos but attack patterns are engaging enough that I am not playing a QTE, I can wander and discover and while there is a sense of danger, I don’t have to inch my way through, terrified that something is gonna spawn, stab my character and take away my progress. I can smile and be curious about the varied NPCs and make up little thought about their inner lives without having the game having to keep a realistic schedule. Also, oddly enough and I hate to say this, no romance. Just two kids chosen by destiny to stop a big bad in a world of magic. (I know there are shippers in every fandom. I don’t see it, so leave me alone). The presentation is beautiful, the gameplay and control design smooth and with no technical issues It feels like the storytelling quirks of all the Zelda games were neatly layered on to make the characters feel more vibrant, while leading with OoT’s narrative style.

It’s odd. As much as I loved OoT as a kid/adult, I didn’t think it a masterpiece…and felt it was a little bit of a sacred cow, even as I have replayed it many times. Yet this new version with its thrilling music, intuitive UI, engaging but constantly moving narrative style and beautiful but whimsical graphics I now get what people meant by this being a masterpiece instead of a very good game. If BOTW was LoZ (first game) fully realized, then this S2 OoT is OoT fully realized. It feels like a memory, it feels like a reunion, it feels like a link to the future. It is the best way to play OoT. What a time to be a Zelda fan.

It's a gorgeous remake. If you're a newcomer to the game, I'd call this a 8.5/10 - with the only issue being how weirdly handhold-y it is. I just finished the Zora's Domain area, and holy crap, it is absurd how much the game spells out exactly what to do. First they put a Zora holding a bottle in around the fish area, then they have the signpost in Zora's fountain literally saying "give an offering to Jabu," then a pool of water directly in front of Jabu, perfect for a fish. Any one of these hints would have been enough, but all 3? Overkill. If you have played the game before, I'd score it an 8, since the handhold-y bits wouldn't bother you (you know exactly what to do anyway). It feels a lot like taking a riding a Rollercoaster you've already ridden before. Very enjoyable, but the thrills are milder. I dock an extra half point because I think the devs could have added more stuff to celebrate the re-release (dev commentaries, concept art, old TV commercials, etc.). I also think the soundtrack is mixed weirdly. Certain instruments are way way too loud, and so the atmosphere in some places isn't as strong as it could be.

Don't miss men's and women's matches for WWE Crown Jewel Championships during the wrestling event.

All products and services featured are independently chosen by editors. However, Billboard may receive a commission on orders placed through its retail links, and the retailer may receive certain auditable data for accounting purposes.

On the road to Survivor Series: WarGames in Houston, the WWE makes a pit stop in Riyadh for an epic night of massive title bouts during Crown Jewel.

The premium live event features four high-profile Raw vs. SmackDown matches, including Roman Reigns taking on Sami Zayn for the WWE Crown Jewel Championship, Liv Morgan goes face-to-face against Chelsea Green for the WWE Women’s Crown Jewel Championship and other bouts. Related Sony's New Hello Kitty Headphones Come With an Ultra-Chic Carrying Case That Deserves Its Own Spotlight The Best Ugly Christmas Sweaters for Music Fans to Shop Online in 2026 Here's Where to Watch Nebraska Cornhuskers vs. Illinois Fighting Illini Live Online Free Explore Billboard See latest videos, charts and news

Crown Jewel happens on Saturday (Nov. 7), beginning at 12 p.m. ET/9 p.m. PT. The event takes place at ANB Arena in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia.

The WWE is known for celebrity sightings, so it’s likely there may be a number of famous actors, actresses and recording artists in attendance, even in Saudi Arabia. Celebrities who have been known to appear at events in the past, include Logan Paul, Cardi B, Mike Tyson and others.

For 2026, Crown Jewel is available to watch on ESPN Unlimited. It’s not a pay-per-view event, but rather you’d need access to ESPN Unlimited to watch all of the WWE action. Although the sports streaming service doesn’t have a free trial to watch Crown Jewel for free, you can sign up for a five-day free trial to DirecTV, which comes with access to ESPN Unlimited.

A subscription to DirecTV gets you access to live TV, local and cable channels at $59.99 for the first month of service ($89.99 per month afterward). In fact, the “Entertainment” package comes with access to ESPN Unlimited to watch Crown Jewel, as well as Disney+ and Hulu at no extra cost. The service even offers a five-day free trial to watch for free, if you sign up now.

You can watch local networks, such as NBC, CBS and FOX, while you can watch many cable networks too, including FX, AMC, A&E, Bravo, Cartoon Network, ESPN, FS1, VH1, Fuse, CNN, Food Network, Lifetime, CNBC, BET, MTV, Paramount Network and many others.

ESPN Unlimited is the official streaming platform for Crown Jewel. A subscription includes instant access for $31.99 per month. However, you can save almost 17% off by purchasing an annual subscription for $319.99 per year. It features every ESPN network, including ESPN, ESPN2, ESPNU, ESPNEWS, ESPN Deportes, SEC Network, ACC Network, ESPN on ABC, ESPN+, SEC Network+ and ACC Network Extra — all in one streaming service.

In addition to live sports, ESPN Unlimited has original programming to stream on demand. While the service doesn’t offer a free trial, you can still watch ESPN Unlimited with a clever workaround to watch online for free. Learn more about how to get a free trial to access ESPN Unlimited here.

In 2026, there are four matches for the WWE premium live event. The main event is men’s and women’s Crown Jewel Championship matches.

Hi, friends! Welcome to Installer No. 151, your guide to the best and Verge-iest stuff in the world. (If you’re new here, welcome, get psyched because it’s officially Bad Holiday Movie Season, and also you can read all the old editions at the Installer homepage.)

This week, I’ve been reading about Andrew Garfield and GTA VI and sexy joke thieves, finally actually watching The Sopranosfor the first time (I know, I know), mourning the end of Salon, reinstalling the new and extremely improved Notion Calendar, testing the new Keurig Alta coffee machine, reviewing the iPad Mini and the touchscreen MacBook Pro, and really thinking about just throwing it all away and buying an Enough Phone.

I also have for you the long-awaited return of a classic Zelda game, a monster movie worth the trek to the theater, a couple of great new phone accessories, awesome retro headphones, and more. Let’s do it.

We talked last week about Readwise Books, the new app that’s a mix of Kindle, Goodreads, and Strava. I’m a longtime fan and user of Readwise Reader, so I wasn’t shocked that I liked the new app a lot, too. And I’ve heard from a bunch of you that you do, too!

I’ve talked to Tristan Homsi, one of the Readwise cofounders, a few times over the years, and have always enjoyed hearing his thoughts about reading, apps, and digital products in general. I asked him to share his homescreen to see if I might be able to get a hint about what he’s reading and the other stuff he thinks is good. (I also asked Daniel Doyon, Readwise’s other cofounder, to share, and he didn’t respond. Daniel is dead to me.)

Here’s Tristan’s homescreen, plus some info on the apps he uses and why:

The phone: iPhone 13 (!).Believe it or not, I chose to buy this relatively outdated phone last year… not because it’s my favorite form factor or anything, but because it’s closer in specs to the median device our Readwise users have and helps me build a bit more user empathy with them. I’m able to catch performance regressions and bugs in Readwise that the developers on our team with Duos and Pro Maxes and such miss.

Let me tell you though, iOS 26 itself does not work amazingly on an iPhone 13. Lots of lagging and janky animations. I am somewhat bought into the conspiracy theory that they make the old phones suck so you buy a new one… not so much intentionally, but rather by virtue of just making each successive iOS use more and more resources, doing fancier and fancier things.​

The wallpaper: My lovely wife and my cat Sophie :)

The apps: Clock, Settings, Google Calendar, Camera, Google Maps, Spotify, Bay Wheels, Oura, Eight Sleep, Auto Eight, BeReal, ChatGPT, Runmeter, Strava, Readwise Highlights, Readwise Reader, Phone, Messages, Safari.

Of course, I have all three Readwise apps front and center. Highlights and Reader as app icons, and Readwise Books in widget form at the top! It’s a great reminder to jump back into your current book (I’m reading Apple in China right now… speaking of Apple!) instead of scrolling twitter (on a later page), and seeing my friends currently reading really makes me want to jump in and join them :)

I also am a huge fan of the somewhat-defunct social app BeReal. It had its moment in 2022, but quite a few of my friends are still on there! You post once a day, can see what your close friends around the world are doing, and most importantly can’t get sucked in for more than two minutes since it limits you to a random photo or two a day! I still post basically every day, and have almost five years of memories in there.

Speaking of getting sucked in, I don’t have it pictured on my home screen, but I also have Clearspace installed, which helps me stay off of all social media.

Some other apps shown on the homescreen: “auto eight” is something I vibecoded to better control my Eight Sleep mattress (lol)… Strava and Runmeter are awesome for tracking my runs… and Bay Wheels is my main mode of commute around San Francisco daily.

I also asked Tristan to share a few things he’s into right now. Here’s what he sent back:

Email installer@theverge.com or message me on Signal — @davidpierce.11 — with your recommendations for anything and everything, and we’ll feature some of our favorites here every week. For even more great recommendations, check out the replies to this post on Threads and this post on Bluesky.

I’ve mentioned Kagi a bunch of times in this newsletter — it has been my default search engine for the last few years, and I can’t recommend it highly enough. (What if Google, but good, you know?) The challenge with Kagi is it costs $10 a month, which is a lot to ask when Google is free. But as of this week, you can now at least try it for yourself, since Kagi will give you 50 free searches before even asking you to sign up. You get 100 more when you do, even before you pay.

If you search a lot, and miss what Google used to be, give Kagi a whirl. I am a very happy customer, and suspect I will be for a long time.

Death Row’s Last Meals Anchor a Museum-Wide Survey of Incarceration Art

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Louisiana State Penitentiary, known as “Angola” for the slave plantation that once occupied its land, lies at the dead end of Highway 66.

Past the gate, the road continues—invitation permitting—through 18,000 acres of penal farmland punctuated by gun towers, barbed-wire enclosures, and incarcerated men picking crops on the so-called farm line. America’s largest maximum-security prison, long notorious for its reliance on solitary confinement, Angola runs on the forgotten.

Gary Tyler, a fiber artist and activist, remembers Angola. He arrived in 1974 at seventeen years old, wrongly convicted of shooting a white student during a segregationist mob’s attack on his school bus. The prison is bound on three sides by bayou, bluffs, and swamp; escaping into one’s mind was easier. His grandmother was a quilter, he said, and fabric could be found or bartered. Time was given. Over four decades inside, including two stints on death row, he got good, even renowned. Craftswomen from across the South came to Angola to find the man behind the textiles making rounds outside. Related Articles Art en Route: Museum of London Eyes Trump Baby Balloon, Art Institute of Chicago Deaccessions Chinese Artworks, and More Other Voices, Other Worlds

“All that time, I never thought of myself as an ‘artist,’” Tyler said at the opening of “Rebirth,” a solo show of his quilted and appliquéd works at the Grand Rapids Art Museum. “I said, ‘Call me a hobbyist.’ Artists were people in galleries, people with an audience.”

“Rebirth” is one of three solo presentations on view in Grand Rapids through January 2027 that showcase art by or about people once incarcerated within the United States. Ray Materson, whose show is subtitled “Metamorphosis,” works in a medium he invented during his prison sentence for a drug-fueled robbery with a toy gun: palm-sized embroideries stitched by hand from the threads of unraveled socks, Americana meets Renaissance miniature.

The third show is The Last Supper, a 1,000-piece ceramic installation by the artist Julie Green, shown in its entirety for only the second time. For twenty years, up until their death in 2021, Green illustrated final-meal choices on ceramic plates painted in cobalt blue and re-fired in her kiln. What does the condemned order on death row? An overflowing bucket of Kentucky Fried Chicken, lobster, steak, burgers and fries, pecan pie. Nothing, sometimes: inmates can refuse or be denied a selection. (Final meal selections are generally a matter of public records.) At a quick glance, these plates might pass for Staffordshire. A closer look could ruin your lunch.

“It’s not so much that these shows are timely,” said Jennifer Wcisel, curator of collections at the Grand Rapids Art Museum. “It’s that the question of what incarceration means to American life is never absent. It’s ongoing.”

But has punishment been so openly advertised since the pre-Civil Rights era? Tennessee’s botched attempt to execute Christa Pike, the only woman on its death row, was followed by a magazine interview with the physician on hand to pronounce her dead (he said the state should try again). The Defense Secretary called for the military’s first execution in six decades—by firing squad—to be livestreamed. The White House launched an online arcade, where in one game a pixelated Border Patrol agent, rounds up “border crossers.”

The art can’t hope to match the volume of cruelty. It aspires to empathy or at least empathy’s catalyst: curiosity. Whose aim does a public execution serve? Within the carceral system, how is dignity doled out?

The Last Supper argues that dignity is a fickle allowance. Texas ended special last meals in 2011, after white supremacist Lawrence Russell Brewer ordered a feast and left it untouched. Set against Green’s terrible, tender plates, the result is hard to defend. Bite-size biographies like “he never had a birthday cake so we ordered a birth-day cake for him” give way to row upon row of “Standard prison fare.” This is dinnerware for a haunted house.

It is also a map of America’s patchwork principles on capital punishment. The Supreme Court barred executing people with intellectual disabilities in 2002, then left the states to define who qualifies, within limits. One plate records an IQ of 61, well below the benchmark of about 70, and a request that the menu not be released to the media. Another records a request that their meal be saved until after the execution.

Everything on view is portraiture, if the word can stretch to cover a spirit given a recognizable shape, even one abridged to a birthday cake. Tyler and Materson work with representational directness in the opposite register from Green. We see faces. Their work can indulge fantasy, the province of the living. One of Tyler’s quilts depicts a former inmate known as Big Toe in the so-called Greek Alps, still wearing the armor from The Life of Jesus Christ, a play produced by the drama club Tyler founded. Portraiture at its best is said to reveal the heart. For centuries it was also a privilege of the powerful, who commissioned likenesses to control how they were seen. Prison inverts that arrangement: the powerful do the watching. But what does surveillance ever capture of its subjects? Insight can’t be taken, only given.

Materson’s intricate embroideries stray in their subjects from baseball to outer space, with the most striking blending imagination and documentation. In Self-Portrait (undated), he sits before a mirror, stitching the very piece the viewer is looking at. Down a receding tunnel, a smaller Materson stitches a smaller Materson, and so on: infinity, or its likeness. The thick black frame that cages his face is made of the same thread as the rest, not impossibly undone.

One quilt late in Tyler’s show features a great crane, the kind that roosted in the wetlands around Angola and sometimes landed in the prison yard. Tyler was released in 2016, after decades of campaigning by activists including Rosa Parks. The crane captures that moment. His soul, he said, sprang skyward on his first step outside. It has yet to descend.

Tyler’s Convict Poker (2025) unfolds at the Angola prison rodeo: a Black man hangs in midair, bucked from a bull. The last of its kind in the country, the event is held twice a year and open to a paying public. Spectators can’t legally bet; the gamble belongs to the prisoners who volunteer. In convict poker, a raging bull is loosed on a mock card game, and the last man still seated wins a prize. Posting this October’s rodeo to Facebook, an attendee wrote: “The announcer said they didn’t run that fast from the PoPo 🚓😂 Watch til the end, prisoners were flying…”

Tyler never rode in the rodeo. But he doesn’t judge. (One of his quilts were raffled at the rodeo and raised $30,000 for the hospice program.) The men who volunteer, he said, are willing “to jeopardize their lives or go through a sense of degradation just to entertain an audience because nobody wants to be forgotten.”

“These are men who may never go home. The announcer mentions their names, and that they are serving life sentences; understand?”

Christmas Flights To Europe Cost $2,000 Each In Economy. Should I Book Now Or Gamble On Award Space?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

I’m faced with a travel dilemma concerning holiday airline tickets and as I evaluate how best to handle it, I thought I would solicit your feedback, dear readers.

We want to spend Christmas in Germany this year and arrive the weekend before Christmas. My wife’s family is having a big Christmas party the weekend before and as many of her family is aging, it’s important that we are there.

My objective, as with every transatlantic trip, is to use points to pay for the ticket, preferably in premium cabin, and even more preferably on the outbound overnight flight to Europe.

But have you checked Christmas fares from the USA to Europe lately? They are through the roof! Last year, I had no trouble getting SAS business class tickets on Christmas Eve to Europe (though from Chicago instead of Los Angeles), returning on New Year’s. This year SAS has nothing and we need to fly earlier anyway…at the same time many others are also trying to go.

Economy class tickets are pushing $2,000 each and I don’t see anything close to saver space on the usual suspects (I’m monitoring space using Atmos, AAdvantage, Aeroplan, Avios, Flying Blue, and MileagePlus, I’m also using tools like Seats.Aero to track award space).

But at this point, there’s really nothing…and I’m not being picky. I’ll take economy class or long layovers, but I’m just not willing to pay 4-5x saver pricing or the current cash price.

I can handle this is one of three ways. First, I can just wait and hope that saver space opens. It’s too early to tell how full these flights will get, but waiting for saver space to open has generally worked in the past with a couple days of flexibility.

Second, I can buy the tickets now in case the price goes up later. If book with United, if the price goes down I can cancel and rebook, baking the residual as a future flight credit. If I wait and do nothing, I may end up spending even more as well get closer to travel because this trip is happening no matter what (God willing…).

The third option would be flying to Germany a week earlier and staying a week later…the luxury of time. But that’s not ideal because I would have to pull the kids out of school (1st and 4th grade) and I’m very busy myself. My wife’s schedule would not allow it – she’s traveling on set dates regardless of cost (and yes, I’ve also been tracking single award seats for her).

I’m less worried about the kids missing school than me trying to work from Germany, which is great for blogging but not so good for the other work that I do.

Am I missing anything? Let me know your thoughts because I’ll feel a lot better once I have something booked, but I’d feel like a sucker if I ended up paying that much for coach tickets….

That O’Hare concourse image is forever associated with Christmas travel, sin’t it?

Join our mailing list for a daily summary of posts! We never sell your info.

Matthew is an avid traveler who calls Los Angeles home. Each year he travels more than 200,000 miles by air and has visited more than 135 countries. Working both in the aviation industry and as a travel consultant, Matthew has been featured in major media outlets around the world and uses his Live and Let's Fly blog to share the latest news in the airline industry, commentary on frequent flyer programs, and detailed reports of his worldwide travel.

My option would be to book a flight with a refundable fare. As you get closer, you still have the option to either rebook a nonrefundable fare that presumably would be cheaper than the refundable fare or, if an award booking does pop open, book the award booking and cancel the refundable fare.

Of course the refundable fare will be more expensive, most likely. There are many times when I’ve found that the refundable fare is not too much more expensive than a really expensive nonrefundable fare. Even when you get close to the actual flight time, there’s minimal increase in price over the nonrefundable fare. Since it’s so expensive to begin with, it doesn’t increase that much by the time you get to the actual flight date

Ugh. I wish I had better advice for you, but for this Christmas and now only 6-7 weeks out… I’d just book whatever you can get and would not count on award space. If some opens up and you end up with $8k in United trip credit – not ideal, but I’m sure it will get spent down. I’m certainly not opposed to missing a day or two of school (similar situation), but a week… eh, starts to get a bit much, and the reality is the kids like being in school.

I guess I’d look to see if booking two one-way cash tickets might result in a cheaper price, and would also look at the google flights grid to see what price you can get on what dates. I’m sure you tried that already…

What about flying into/via another market? Sometimes, it can be cheaper to go to BCN / CDG etc. and do an overnight there or catch a late connecting flight / train.

Southwest Airlines Passenger Says Flight Attendant Quit After Crew Fight—Pilot Called It ‘Personality Differences’

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A Southwest Airlines passenger says a flight attendant quit after a verbal argument with fellow cabin crew in front of the whole plane. She says passengers were then told a flight attendant was sick—before the pilot came on and offered a different explanation: “personality differences.” This was Southwest flight 557 from Dallas Love Field to Kansas City on Friday, November 6. The passenger began complaining while the flight was still on the ground. By 4:39 p.m., she identified the flight number and airport and said they’d been waiting two hours. A screenshot of her account shared on Reddit adds, “ONE QUIT ONE ASKED TO LEAVE!” She describes the argument taking place in front of passengers, followed by an announcement that the flight attendants were sick. Then, according to her, the pilot said: NOT SURE WHY THEY SAID THAT BUT WE HAD PERSONALITY DIFFERENCES @SouthwestAir FLIGHT 557 the flight attendants were full out arguing with each other. They announced a flight attendant was sick AFTER WE ALL WATCHED THE FIGHT! Now the pilot says “PERSONALITY DIFFERENCES” WHAT THE ACTUAL HECK — Jenni From De Bloc (@dualczn) November 6, 2026 She also acknowledges a maintenance issue, but says it was resolved about ten minutes after boarding. She attributes the extended wait to the crew dispute. The Boeing 737-700, registration N255WN, was scheduled to leave Dallas at 2:25 p.m. It took off at 5:12 p.m. and landed in Kansas City at 6:16 p.m., 2 hours and 21 minutes late. Losing a required flight attendant means finding a replacement before the plane can leave. And if crew members can’t work together, sending them off to handle an emergency together would have been… problematic.. Last year, United replaced an entire cabin crew after two flight attendants got into a dispute in Des Moines. Replacing people may be necessary. Arguing in front of customers, then giving them an explanation the pilot contradicts, is a also a choice. The Southwest aircraft continued from Kansas City to Nashville and then Knoxville that evening. It didn’t land in Knoxville until 10:47 p.m. Eastern, two hours and 12 minutes late. This was Southwest flight 557 from Dallas Love Field to Kansas City on Friday, November 6. The passenger began complaining while the flight was still on the ground. By 4:39 p.m., she identified the flight number and airport and said they’d been waiting two hours.

A screenshot of her account shared on Reddit adds, “ONE QUIT ONE ASKED TO LEAVE!” She describes the argument taking place in front of passengers, followed by an announcement that the flight attendants were sick. Then, according to her, the pilot said:

NOT SURE WHY THEY SAID THAT BUT WE HAD PERSONALITY DIFFERENCES

@SouthwestAir FLIGHT 557 the flight attendants were full out arguing with each other. They announced a flight attendant was sick AFTER WE ALL WATCHED THE FIGHT! Now the pilot says “PERSONALITY DIFFERENCES” WHAT THE ACTUAL HECK — Jenni From De Bloc (@dualczn) November 6, 2026

She also acknowledges a maintenance issue, but says it was resolved about ten minutes after boarding. She attributes the extended wait to the crew dispute.

The Boeing 737-700, registration N255WN, was scheduled to leave Dallas at 2:25 p.m. It took off at 5:12 p.m. and landed in Kansas City at 6:16 p.m., 2 hours and 21 minutes late.

Losing a required flight attendant means finding a replacement before the plane can leave. And if crew members can’t work together, sending them off to handle an emergency together would have been… problematic.. Last year, United replaced an entire cabin crew after two flight attendants got into a dispute in Des Moines.

Replacing people may be necessary. Arguing in front of customers, then giving them an explanation the pilot contradicts, is a also a choice. The Southwest aircraft continued from Kansas City to Nashville and then Knoxville that evening. It didn’t land in Knoxville until 10:47 p.m. Eastern, two hours and 12 minutes late.

PS6 doesn't need more power, ex-PlayStation boss says

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

"What the company needs to do is do what it's never done," Shawn Layden says

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Former PlayStation boss Shawn Layden doesn't think the inevitable PS6 needs to be more powerful to be a success. While new console generations offer less drastic technical improvements each time, Layden reckons "true innovation" would come from making the next-gen machine affordable.

Speaking to The Trophy Room podcast, Layden recalls how past generations offered "something mind-blowing" every time a new console came out. "The jump from PS1 to PS2 was incredible," he says, "and the jump from compact disc to DVD." Even the PS4 was "completely networked" and Sony still managed to offer the console for $399 at the time.

Meanwhile, rumors are that the PS6 will come with "even more ray tracing and more teraflops." That might sound impressive to tech wizards, but to Layden and the average player, "these are differences only dogs can hear."

"I think we have enough power in the machine. The innovation needs to come not from making PS6 for $1,200 - it's to make another PS5 for $300. Now, that would be true innovation," he explains. Layden's comments are particularly timely right now as the PS5 and Xbox Series X|S are much more expensive today than they were at launch, six years ago. "This is the first platform that I've seen that has gone up in price over time."

Those rising console costs come from the ongoing RAM shortage, as AI data centers gobble up components that are used in almost every other consumer device, from consoles to smart phones. For Layden, these price points block a large crowd of potential buyers from ever entering the door - a crowd that would've traditionally jumped in at the end of the generation for a cheaper asking price.

"What the company needs to do is do what it's never done, which is don't iterate the console," he says. "Make it smaller, make it cheaper, do all the things you can around that. It's more than enough power to build the kind of games all of us want to play."

Sony has "a good chance of winning" with a PS6 handheld, former PlayStation boss says, but only if there's no home console launch, too

Wildlife Silhouettes and the Power of Shape

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Wildlife Silhouettes and the Power of Shape

Explore how posture, shape, light, and composition can help wildlife photographers create striking backlit imagery that tells a powerful story.

Wildlife photography often emphasizes detail. Photographers work to capture the texture of feathers, patterns in an animal’s coat, expressions in its eyes, and the small features that reveal its character. Silhouette photography takes a different approach. Instead of relying on those details, it reduces an animal to its outline, using shape, posture, and positioning to communicate the subject. The result is a more graphic, interpretive image that asks viewers to recognize the animal through its form rather than its appearance.

Creating compelling wildlife silhouettes involves more than placing an animal in front of a bright sunset. The subject needs a recognizable outline, its posture should contribute to the image, and the background must provide enough contrast to make its shape clear. Composition and timing also matter more because small changes in an animal’s position can dramatically alter the photograph. Understanding these elements helps photographers recognize opportunities where removing detail can make a wildlife image more expressive.

A successful silhouette depends on how easily the viewer can identify the subject and interpret its position. Without visible detail, the outline becomes the primary source of information. Body proportions, head position, and distinctive anatomical features all contribute to whether an animal remains recognizable. A silhouette that communicates its subject immediately is generally more effective than one that leaves the viewer guessing.

Some animals and poses naturally lend themselves to this approach. An elk with clearly defined antlers, a fox with its ears raised, or a bird with its wings extended has a distinctive outline that remains identifiable even when rendered entirely dark. By contrast, an animal facing directly toward the camera may lose much of its recognizable shape. Its body can appear compressed, and important features may overlap.

A strong silhouette should also contribute something to the photograph beyond simply creating a dark subject. It might emphasize an animal’s scale against a large landscape, highlight a particular behavior, or simplify an otherwise busy scene. The most effective images use the silhouette as a deliberate storytelling choice rather than treating it as an exposure effect.

Before photographing an animal as a silhouette, consider which anatomical features make it recognizable. Different species have distinctive profiles, and understanding those features can help you anticipate the most effective shooting angle. For mammals, this might be the shape of the head, the length of the legs, the position of the ears, or features such as antlers and horns. For birds, the bill, neck, tail, and wing shape often provide the most important identifying characteristics.

The angle of the animal can change how these features appear. A broadside position often provides a recognizable profile because the body, head, and legs appear as separate shapes. A three-quarter angle can add depth, particularly when the animal turns its head toward the camera. Front-facing positions can also work, but they often rely on distinctive features to keep the outline from looking indistinct.

Birds offer a wide range of possibilities because their silhouettes change so dramatically with wing position. A bird in flight with its wings fully extended has a very different outline from one gliding with its wings angled downward. The shape of the wings can also help identify a species, with long, narrow wings, broad rounded wings, and separated flight feathers each creating different visual patterns.

Studying animal profiles and observing wildlife in the field can help you recognize these opportunities. Instead of focusing only on whether an animal is facing the right direction, look for positions that reveal its defining characteristics. A slight turn of the head or a change in ear position can be enough to transform an ordinary outline into a recognizable portrait.

When fine detail disappears, posture becomes one of the most important ways to communicate an animal’s character. A standing animal with its head lowered may create a relatively unremarkable shape, while the same animal raising its head, stretching, or interacting with another individual can produce a much more expressive image. The silhouette should ideally show something about the animal, not just document its presence.

Behavior can provide some of the most interesting opportunities. An elk bugling with its head raised, a bear standing on its hind legs, or a bird extending its wings during a display all create recognizable gestures. Even smaller movements can work well. A fox listening with its ears pointed forward, or a heron poised with its neck extended, can create a distinctive profile without requiring dramatic action.

Anticipating these moments is often more effective than reacting after they happen. Wildlife photographers already spend considerable time learning animal behavior to predict where subjects will move and when interesting activity might occur. The same preparation applies to silhouettes. If you know a bird is likely to take flight from a particular perch, or that an animal regularly pauses on an exposed ridge, you can prepare for the moment rather than scrambling to adjust your position after it begins.

Continuous shooting can help when the opportunity involves movement, but a burst of frames does not guarantee the strongest composition. Watch for the positions that communicate the behavior most clearly. For a bird in flight, that might mean waiting for the wings to reach full extension. For a mammal, it could be a moment when its head and limbs create a particularly expressive gesture. The goal is to capture the most meaningful position, not simply the most frames.

The simplicity of a silhouette makes compositional decisions especially noticeable. With fewer details competing for attention, the animal’s placement, direction, and relationship with the surrounding space carry much of the photograph’s visual weight.

Consider the direction in which the animal is looking or moving. Leaving open space in front of a subject can create a sense of movement and give the viewer somewhere to follow its gaze. A bird flying toward the open side of the frame, for example, generally feels less confined than one positioned directly against the edge. Similarly, an animal looking toward an open horizon can create a sense of anticipation or connection with the landscape.

The animal’s size within the frame also changes the story. A relatively large silhouette emphasizes anatomy and posture, making it easier to appreciate the subject’s shape. A smaller animal surrounded by open sky or landscape can communicate scale, distance, and isolation. This wider approach can be particularly effective when the environment is an important part of the photograph.

Natural lines can provide useful compositional structure. A ridgeline, shoreline, or distant tree line can anchor an animal within the scene and create a visual connection between the subject and its surroundings. Experiment with both tight and wide compositions when the situation allows. A tighter frame emphasizes the animal, while a wider image can reveal more about its place in the landscape.

Silhouette photography depends on contrast between the subject and its surroundings. Sunrise and sunset provide familiar opportunities because the sky is often much brighter than the landscape. Positioning an animal against a bright section of sky can create a dark outline while retaining the colors and tonal transitions of the surrounding light.

The sun does not necessarily need to be visible in the frame. A colorful band of light near the horizon, a bright opening between clouds, or a softly illuminated sky can all provide an effective background. Other lighting conditions can work as well. Bright overcast skies, open water, reflections, and distant illuminated areas of landscape may provide enough contrast to isolate an animal.

Think about the background as an active part of the composition rather than simply a source of brightness. A relatively uniform sky often works well because it allows the animal’s shape to stand out. More complex backgrounds can introduce atmosphere and context, but they may compete with the subject if they contain too many contrasting elements.

Light quality and direction change as the sun rises or sets, so the best background may be available only briefly. Identify promising positions before the animal reaches them, and watch how the light changes across the scene. A slight shift in the animal’s position or the sky’s color can produce a noticeably different result.

Even a distinctive animal can become difficult to identify when its outline merges with the surrounding environment. Branches, grasses, rocks, and other animals can obscure important features, particularly the legs, ears, antlers, and wings. Because a silhouette removes most internal detail, these overlaps matter more than they would in a conventional wildlife portrait.

Before taking the photograph, scan the entire outline. Check whether the animal’s legs are distinguishable, whether its head and ears are clearly defined, and whether important features are lost against nearby objects. With birds, pay particular attention to the wings and tail. A bird with its wings tucked tightly against its body may appear as a much simpler shape than one with its feathers spread.

Changing your position is often the easiest way to improve separation. Moving a short distance to one side can shift a branch out of the animal’s outline or create a gap between its legs. A lower shooting position may place the subject against the sky rather than vegetation, while a higher position can sometimes remove distracting foreground elements. These adjustments are especially useful in wetlands, grasslands, and wooded environments.

Multiple animals introduce another challenge. When individuals overlap, their silhouettes can merge into a single shape that is difficult to interpret. This does not mean you should always avoid groups. A group of birds in flight can create a compelling pattern, and animals gathered together may communicate social behavior. The important distinction is whether the arrangement remains visually understandable. Waiting for individuals to separate or for their positions to change can make the difference between a confusing cluster and a deliberate composition.

Exposing for a silhouette requires a different approach from a traditional wildlife portrait. Rather than preserving detail in the animal’s shadows, the goal is to retain important background information while letting the subject fall into darkness. This means exposing for the brighter surroundings instead of trying to balance the entire scene.

Automatic exposure modes can sometimes make this difficult. When a camera detects a large dark subject against a bright background, its metering system may attempt to brighten the scene. The result can be an animal with visible shadow detail instead of a solid silhouette. Exposure compensation provides a straightforward way to address this. Reducing exposure can keep the subject dark while preserving the sky’s colors and tonal information.

Manual exposure offers more control, particularly when the background brightness remains relatively consistent while the animal moves through the frame. You can establish an exposure based on the sky and maintain those settings rather than allowing the camera to continually adjust for changes in the composition. Spot or center-weighted metering can also help, depending on where you place the metering area and how your camera interprets the scene.

The histogram helps you evaluate exposure, especially when photographing colorful skies. Overexposing the brightest areas can remove the subtle color transitions that make the background interesting. Review the histogram and highlight warnings to determine whether you are retaining the tones you want. At the same time, don’t worry about preserving shadow detail in the animal if you intend to create a true silhouette.

Shutter speed remains important because wildlife behavior doesn’t become less unpredictable just because the subject is dark. A bird taking flight still needs a fast enough shutter speed to preserve the shape of its wings, and a running mammal may require a similarly fast exposure to prevent motion blur. If the animal is stationary, you may have more flexibility, but it is still worth accounting for small movements such as turning its head or shifting its weight.

Autofocus can become more challenging when the subject is dark against a bright background. Depending on the camera and scene, the autofocus system may have fewer visible details to identify. Tracking modes and subject detection can still help, but watch how reliably the camera follows the animal. In some situations, focusing on the subject before it moves into the brightest part of the background may be more effective. Continuous autofocus and burst shooting can help with unpredictable movement, but they work best when paired with an understanding of the animal’s likely path.

Although the animal itself is often rendered as a dark shape, the background can bring considerable color and atmosphere to a silhouette photograph. Sunrise and sunset provide familiar warm tones, but cooler twilight colors can work just as well. Deep blues, soft purples, and muted pastels can create a quieter mood, while reflections and layers of color can add depth without distracting from the subject.

Negative space gives the animal room to stand apart and allows the viewer to focus on its shape. This works especially well when photographing a small animal against a large sky or open landscape. The surrounding space becomes part of the story, communicating scale and emphasizing how the subject fits into its environment.

Minimalist compositions often work well because they reduce competing elements. A single bird against a colorful sky, an elk standing on a distant ridge, or a fox crossing an open field can create a strong image with very little visual information. However, minimalism does not mean every photograph needs only one subject. A small number of carefully arranged elements can create balance, rhythm, or visual contrast.

Reflections offer another way to add visual interest while keeping the composition simple. A bird standing in shallow water may produce a second shape beneath its body, creating symmetry or a repeating pattern. A calm lake can also provide a contrasting surface, depending on the direction of the light. These compositions can be particularly effective when the reflection remains recognizable and adds something to the overall arrangement.

Another way to use minimalism is to move in closer and create a tighter portrait, emphasizing the animal’s outline and the details that define its shape. At this distance, subtle textures and contours become more prominent, adding visual interest while keeping the composition simple. This is another time when I play with my exposure to decide if I want a flat silhouette or one with more information in the darkened areas.

Silhouettes, or backlit images, aren’t always the best approach for every wildlife encounter. Many situations require an animal’s coloration, markings, expression, or visible behavior to tell the story. A close portrait that reveals the texture of fur or the details of a bird’s plumage may communicate far more than a dark outline. Deciding whether to photograph a subject as a silhouette should come down to what the image is meant to convey.

When shape or gesture is the most interesting part of the scene, removing detail can strengthen the photograph. A bird spreading its wings against a sunset, an elk calling from a ridgeline, or a mammal looking toward an open horizon can communicate its presence through posture alone. The viewer does not need to see every detail to understand the moment. Instead, the simplified form encourages them to focus on the animal’s movement and relationship with the environment.

Most of all, silhouettes can add variety to a wildlife portfolio. Photographers naturally spend considerable time pursuing sharp, detailed images, particularly when working with long telephoto lenses and tracking fast-moving subjects. A silhouette offers a different visual approach, emphasizing composition and light over revealing every feature. It can help tell a broader story about an encounter or location, especially when paired with more traditional wildlife portraits.

When conditions allow, photograph a scene both ways. A traditional exposure may reveal the animal’s markings and expression, while a silhouette may create a more graphic, atmospheric interpretation of the same moment. Comparing the two approaches can help you decide which communicates the scene most effectively.

Ultimately, wildlife silhouette photography is about recognizing the visual strength of an animal’s shape. Distinctive anatomy, expressive posture, thoughtful composition, and suitable light all contribute to an image that remains compelling without the details normally associated with wildlife photography. Sometimes, revealing less about an animal allows its shape, movement, and place in the landscape to tell a more compelling story.

Bitcoin holders have received eCash — What can you do with it?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin’s eCash fork gives eligible holders an equivalent balance of ECX, but questions remain over where they can trade the tokens and what they can do with them.

Somewhere between the Binance hack and the US midterms, Paul Sztorc’s long-awaited Bitcoin fork, known as the eCash network, went live.

On Oct. 29, Sztorc, the founder of LayerTwo Labs and the driving force behind the Bitcoin Drivechain proposals BIP300 and BIP301 , launched a separate Bitcoin fork designed to bring payments, privacy and other applications to a network built from Bitcoin’s code.

“We need to actively sabotage the ‘degrowth’ policies killing Bitcoin,” Sztorc tells Magazine.

The hard fork gives eligible Bitcoin holders an interesting windfall: their equivalent BTC balance in the new chain’s native token, ECX.

So what benefits does eCash bring to developers, and what can Bitcoin HODLers actually do with their new coins?

Developed by Sztorc’s LayerTwo Labs, eCash is a separate hard fork of Bitcoin born out of Sztorc’s frustration with some of the “complacency narratives” and “cult-like toxicity” he believes has been holding Bitcoin back.

Sztorc dismisses criticism of Drivechains’ security. Source: archive.ecash

Its main innovation is Drivechains, a system that gives developers freedom to build independent sidechains with different features without having to make changes to Bitcoin’s base-layer protocol (or in this case, eCash’s base layer).

Developers can experiment with different applications for payments, privacy, digital assets and prediction markets, and miners can “maximize their revenues” by earning additional transaction fees.

Five sidechains launched alongside eCash’s mainnet: BitNames, BitAssets, Thunder, Truthcoin and zSide, with two more, the quantum-resistant Photon and decentralized exchange CoinShift, still to come.

Related: Blockchain developer Paul Sztorc announces BTC hard fork eCash

Sztorc has spent over a decade advocating for Drivechains and believes that Bitcoin’s notoriously conservative development approach has held back its potential.

“Anything that isn’t maximizing network usage is simply a distraction,” he says. “Users, the ones who actually pay transaction fees to miners, will win in the long run.”

The eCash fork took place at Bitcoin block 973,728, and anyone who controlled their private keys at the snapshot received an equivalent ECX balance.

“If you held the keys to your BTC at that exact block, you now own the exact same amount of ECX,” LayerTwo Labs tells Magazine. “It is that simple, and you can access them right now.”

Like beauty, simplicity is in the eye of the beholder, and Bitcoiners who self-custody their coins will need to use a wallet that supports eCash and carefully follow its process to separate the ECX from their BTC.

Because the two networks share transaction history, an unprotected transaction on one chain could be replayed on the other.

“Never move your BTC first, as an ordinary, unprotected BTC spend can easily be replayed on the ECX network,” LayerTwo Labs warns. Replay controls are built into the protocol, it says, but the company advises holders to move ECX first, before spending BTC.

Anyone keeping BTC on a centralized exchange is “at their mercy regarding distribution,” and should check whether their exchange supports the fork or has announced an ECX distribution.

Accessing ECX isn’t the same thing as being able to sell it and the tokens are not yet widely available on larger crypto exchanges. Depending on which country you are in, there can be tax implications from Bitcoin forks, which are worth looking up before filing your next tax return.

NeoxEX said it opened native ECX deposits, withdrawals and trading on Oct. 29, with ECX/USDT, ECX/USDC and ECX/BTC pairs, while CoinW announced plans to distribute ECX to eligible Bitcoin holders, with deposits, withdrawals and trading to be announced at a later date.

Orca, a decentralized exchange on Solana, lets users swap USDC for wrapped ECX (wECX) through a liquidity pool. That gives holders another way to trade exposure to ECX, but it involves a wrapped token on a different blockchain rather than native ECX trading on the eCash network.

Related: Binance, Polymarket seek Polygon hard fork to recover $6.8M in stolen crypto

How much are the tokens worth? CoinMarketCap placed ECX at $32.63, with around $355,000 in 24-hour trading volume as of Nov. 5, while CoinGecko shows the token as a preview listing, with no price yet available and no tracked exchange markets. Crypto market data provider Kaiko says it does not track ECX, which limits the availability of independent market data.

LayerTwo Labs says early trading saw ECX fluctuate between $40 and $50, but it did not provide supporting order book depth or trading volume figures, pointing instead to the Orca pool’s total value locked as a way for users to assess available liquidity.

eCash is $32.63 on CoinMarketCap as of Nov. 5

“If our fork replicates even a fraction of [Bitcoin Cash] BCH’s initial performance,” Sztorc says, “the liquidity will be staggering,” adding that anyone concerned about low liquidity can hold their coins and decide later.

“We are actively adding new exchanges and trading pairs, so the user experience will only improve,” he says. “Otherwise, you’re welcome to take your free money and leave.”

Sztorc says the value of eCash will ultimately depend on whether developers build useful applications and users are willing to pay for them. Paying customers and transaction fee revenue are the project’s main measures of success during its first year, he says.

He’s not concerned about criticism of Drivechains’ security model, especially the “superstition” that miners could coordinate to steal funds held on sidechains.

“If you genuinely believe miners will torch their own billion-dollar operations for a short-term theft on an L2, then you shouldn’t trust Bitcoin’s L1 either,” he says.

So for now, eCash gives Bitcoiners a new asset and developers a new playground — but whether ECX becomes much more than that will depend on whether anyone actually wants to use it.

It means he was not selected by Zinedine Zidane for France’s Nations League games against Italy and Turkey. This gives Arsenal a chance to continue working on his recovery. He will not be travelling for international games or playing in competitive matches.

Arteta has made it clear he will respect whatever decision Zidane makes regarding Saliba’s availability but acknowledges that he needs some game time after being out for so long.

This means he will be able to continue to be monitored by the club at their London Colney training ground. He won’t be able to return to play in international games ahead of his first appearance for the club.

Arsenal will be hoping that he can use the extra time to get himself ready for the next set of games, including a trip to Newcastle before playing Borussia Dortmund and Manchester City at home.

Arteta will be keen to have Saliba back in contention but will be focused on getting him back at the right time.

There is some immediate optimism on Konsa’s fitness for the game against Hull. But more importantly, Saliba has now progressed to a point where he can be included in the planning process.

Solana’s price decline below $90 has put its recovery prospects at risk, but a potential bullish signal is emerging beneath the ongoing sell-off. While SOL continues to record lower lows, its RSI is moving in the opposite direction, hinting that bearish momentum may be losing strength.

At the same time, 12 consecutive sessions without net inflows into spot Solana ETFs highlight weakening institutional demand. This creates a critical battle between fading selling pressure and a lack of fresh capital. Could this RSI divergence signal Solana’s next rebound, or is another leg lower still ahead?

Solana is trading near $89.90 after breaking below an important support area. The decline follows a broader correction from its October highs, with the token continuing to form lower highs and lower lows.

The latest breakdown also reflects weakening buying interest. SOL has struggled to regain its short-term moving averages, while the broader price structure continues to favour sellers. The loss of support has further weakened the recovery attempt, leaving the token vulnerable to continued volatility.

Institutional flows are adding another layer of pressure to Solana’s recovery prospects. According to the latest market update, spot Solana ETFs have recorded no net inflows for 12 consecutive trading sessions.

JUST IN: 🇺🇸 US spot crypto ETF outflows on Nov 6.• 📉 Bitcoin ($BTC): -$412M• 📉 Ethereum ($ETH): -$93M• 📉 Solana ($SOL): -$25M pic.twitter.com/Muj58z2e4E

The reported data shows that Bitwise and other ETF clients sold approximately $25.08 million worth of SOL in the latest session. The extended absence of positive flows suggests that institutional demand has weakened during the ongoing correction.

ETF activity remains an important indicator of investor appetite, particularly as institutional products provide another route for traditional investors to gain exposure to Solana. A return to consistent inflows could help improve market confidence, while continued outflows may make a sustained recovery harder to establish.

Despite the continued decline, Solana’s technical indicators are showing a potentially encouraging development. Its price is making lower lows, while the RSI is moving higher, creating a bullish divergence.

$SOL is going down, but RSI is trending upThis is what happens when spot sellers are exhausted And every time, seller exhaustion brings a big pump pic.twitter.com/KkCRLxJdwf

This pattern suggests that selling momentum may be weakening even though the price remains under pressure. Such divergences can emerge near the later stages of a correction, when sellers begin losing strength and buyers gradually return.

However, RSI divergence does not guarantee an immediate reversal. SOL still needs stronger buying participation and a meaningful improvement in its price structure to confirm that the downtrend is losing control.

If the divergence develops into a broader recovery signal, it could create an opportunity for Solana to stabilise after its recent losses.

Solana’s near-term outlook remains cautious as weak ETF flows and a deteriorating price structure continue to weigh on the token. The lack of institutional demand also raises questions about how quickly buyers could return following the recent breakdown.

However, the improving RSI presents a potential counter-signal. If selling pressure continues to fade and spot demand recovers, SOL could attempt to reverse its recent losses.

The next phase will depend on whether this technical divergence attracts fresh buying interest or simply provides a temporary pause in the broader decline. For now, Solana’s recovery remains uncertain, but weakening selling momentum offers a reason to watch for a possible change in trend.
